_source line 6271 · 2026-05-18T08:14:04.638Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HcpfZqvRMsQxfAxW6VoBS5`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}
5	Cross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.
6	Per-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest classifier used throughout the main experiments ($\Delta < 0.02$\,pp), and even in the best case (logistic regression) leaves a residual gap relative to within-strategy performance (${\sim}0.83$), ; a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is therefore needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.
7	
8	\paragraph{Strategy-unknown deployment.}
9	In strategy-unknown settings, naive transfer on d1--d3 yields only 45.9\%, and d1/d2 predictions may invert.
10	A two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\,pp over LOO, though per-model gains vary (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\,pp vs.\ Pythia $+$8.0\,pp).
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nCross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.\nPer-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest classifier used throughout the main experiments ($\\Delta < 0.02$\\,pp), and even in the best case (logistic regression) leaves a residual gap relative to within-strategy performance (${\\sim}0.83$), ; a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is therefore needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-unknown deployment.}\nIn strategy-unknown settings, naive transfer on d1--d3 yields only 45.9\\%, and d1/d2 predictions may invert.\nA two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp over LOO, though per-model gains vary (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\nPractical deployment requires either strategy metadata or learned routing.\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\\% across all nine model--strategy settings), inflating headline accuracy.\nCross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled vs.\\ ecological setting.}\nThe primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination where models train on mixed corpora with prior-generation outputs at unknown proportions.\nDomain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\\%) matches standard CV (72.0\\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain_bias}).\nCross-scale experiments (7--8B, \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show weaker but above-chance signal; a base-model control suggests instruction-tuning regularizes degradation.\nSegment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open; in particular, partial mixing (accumulation), where a single document contains fragments from different recursive depths, is a key open question, as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nDeployment on heterogeneous web corpora requires model- and domain-specific calibration.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nEmpirically, the self-model configuration does not yield the highest accuracy (Pythia 78.7\\% vs.\\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\\%), ; the overlap therefore does not confer a systematic advantage.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nIndependent replication with seed=43 yields 77.15\\% (95\\% CI [76.4, 78.15], $\\Delta{=}{-}1.05$\\,pp vs.\\ baseline); cross-seed transfer retains 76.76\\% ($\\Delta{=}{-}1.30$\\,pp vs.\\ within-seed avg), ; depth signal is thus inherent to the recursive process rather than specific LoRA weights (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).\nTop-5 feature importance is identical across seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01SAHgKbQpnoXMC9zinRjQGZ`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	As large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\to$data$\to$model loop arises, yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.
3	We formalize \emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.
4	A Random Forest over 19 perplexity-based features achieves $\approx$69--86\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\%), with perplexity tail statistics emerging as consistent depth markers; a parsimonious subset of 5 features retains 97.6\% of full performance.
5	We define the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for the best-characterized family, and show that cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\% (2.7$\times$ chance).
6	Systematic generalization across eight axes (e.g., cross-scale with 7B models at 70.2\%, prompted generation at 69.5\%, cross-domain, and LoRA-rank ablation) confirms that depth signal is robust across model families but strategy-dependent, residing in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text (zero-shot LLMs: 30--36\%, 43\,pp below RF).
7	\end{abstract}
8	
```

_source line 6288 · 2026-05-18T08:15:10.779Z_

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nAs large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\\to$data$\\to$model loop arises, yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.\nWe formalize \\emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.\nA Random Forest over 19 perplexity-based features achieves $\\approx$69--86\\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\\%), with perplexity tail statistics emerging as consistent depth markers; a parsimonious subset of 5 features retains 97.6\\% of full performance.\nWe define the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for the best-characterized family, and show that cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance).\nSystematic generalization across eight axes (e.g., cross-scale with 7B models at 70.2\\%, prompted generation at 69.5\\%, cross-domain, and LoRA-rank ablation) confirms that depth signal is robust across model families but strategy-dependent, residing in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text (zero-shot LLMs: 30--36\\%, 43\\,pp below RF).\n\\end{abstract}\n",

650	Top features are identical to BOS-seeded (\texttt{p90\_ppl} first); the same perplexity tail erosion mechanism operates under prompted conditions.
651	
652	\subsection{Extended Discussion}
653	\label{app:extended_discussion}
654	
655	\paragraph{Depth-graduated curation.}
656	Current data curation operates on a binary human-vs-machine distinction \cite{dsir2023,qurating2024}.
657	Recursive generation depth estimation enables a finer-grained alternative: retain low-depth generations (d1) whose distributional properties remain close to the human source, while down-weighting or filtering high-depth text (d3+) where tail erosion has degraded quality.
658	The 70--85\% in-distribution accuracy and $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ suggest depth-graduated filtering is feasible within known model--strategy configurations, though the d1/d2 boundary inversion under cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) means reliable curation requires either known decoding strategy metadata or an upstream strategy-identification step; a pilot study (Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}) yields an inconclusive RF-vs-unfiltered comparison ($p>0.4$), though RF-filtered subsets significantly outperform size-matched random subsampling ($p<0.001$--$0.02$); large-scale validation remains open.
659	Importance weighting \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} could further enable continuous depth-aware data selection rather than discrete thresholding.
660	
661	\paragraph{Toward a provenance toolkit.}
662	Depth estimation complements existing provenance methods along orthogonal axes.
663	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} provides high-confidence attribution when the generator cooperates but is inapplicable to unwatermarked text.
664	Source attribution \cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.
665	Depth estimation characterizes \emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.
666	A layered provenance system combining these three signals (watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth) could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.
667	
```

</details>

55	Detection features degrade under domain shift \cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.
56	
57	\section*{Ethics Statement}
58	
59	This work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.
60	No human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.
61	Our method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.
62	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.

      "content": "Detection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.",

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nCross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.\nPer-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest classifier used throughout the main experiments ($\\Delta < 0.02$\\,pp), and even in the best case (logistic regression) leaves a residual gap relative to within-strategy performance (${\\sim}0.83$); a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is therefore needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-unknown deployment.}\nIn strategy-unknown settings, naive transfer on d1--d3 yields only 45.9\\%, and d1/d2 predictions may invert.\nA two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp over LOO, though per-model gains vary (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\nPractical deployment requires either strategy metadata or learned routing.\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\\% across all nine model--strategy settings), inflating headline accuracy.\nCross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled vs.\\ ecological setting.}\nThe primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination where models train on mixed corpora with prior-generation outputs at unknown proportions.\nDomain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\\%) matches standard CV (72.0\\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain_bias}).\nCross-scale experiments (7--8B, \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show weaker but above-chance signal; a base-model control suggests instruction-tuning regularizes degradation.\nSegment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open; in particular, partial mixing (accumulation), where a single document contains fragments from different recursive depths, is a key open question, as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nDeployment on heterogeneous web corpora requires model- and domain-specific calibration.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nEmpirically, the self-model configuration does not yield the highest accuracy (Pythia 78.7\\% vs.\\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\\%), ; the overlap therefore does not confer a systematic advantage.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nIndependent replication with seed=43 yields 77.15\\% (95\\% CI [76.4, 78.15], $\\Delta{=}{-}1.05$\\,pp vs.\\ baseline); cross-seed transfer retains 76.76\\% ($\\Delta{=}{-}1.30$\\,pp vs.\\ within-seed avg), ; depth signal is thus inherent to the recursive process rather than specific LoRA weights (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).\nTop-5 feature importance is identical across seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/section_appendix.tex",
  "old_string": "A layered provenance system combining these three signals (watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth) could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.",
  "new_string": "A layered provenance system combining these three signals (watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth) could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.\n\n\\subsection{Computational Resources and Reproducibility}\n\\label{app:compute}\n\n\\paragraph{Hardware.}\nAll experiments were conducted on 2$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\\,GB) GPUs locally and 2$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\\,GB) on cloud instances.\nTotal computation is approximately 200 GPU$\\cdot$hours, covering the $3 \\times 3$ generation matrix (LoRA fine-tuning + sampling at each depth), feature extraction, depth extension to d5, 7--8B QLoRA experiments, and all ablations.\n\n\\paragraph{Software.}\nKey library versions: PyTorch 2.x, Transformers 4.x, PEFT 0.x, scikit-learn 1.x.\nExact versions will be pinned in the released code repository.\n\n\\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}\nLoRA hyperparameters ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, dropout $0.05$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, 3 epochs) follow \\citet{hu2022lora}.\nThe Random Forest classifier uses scikit-learn defaults (100 trees, Gini criterion, no max depth) without hyperparameter search; all reported results use 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\n\n\\paragraph{Inference cost.}\nFeature extraction and classification require no GPU.\nInference takes approximately 2 minutes per 5{,}000 samples on a single CPU core (reference-model forward pass + RF prediction), enabling deployment without specialized hardware."
}
```

</details>

        "oldLines": 3,
        "newStart": 664,
        "newLines": 22,
        "lines": [
          " Source attribution \\cite{uchendu2020_attribution} identifies the generating model but not the generation history.",
          " Depth estimation characterizes \\emph{iterative reuse} regardless of generator cooperation or identity.",
          " A layered provenance system combining these three signals (watermark presence, source model, and recursive generation depth) could support more nuanced data auditing than any single method.",
          "+",
          "+\\subsection{Computational Resources and Reproducibility}",
          "+\\label{app:compute}",
          "+",
          "+\\paragraph{Hardware.}",
          "+All experiments were conducted on 2$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\\,GB) GPUs locally and 2$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\\,GB) on cloud instances.",
          "+Total computation is approximately 200 GPU$\\cdot$hours, covering the $3 \\times 3$ generation matrix (LoRA fine-tuning + sampling at each depth), feature extraction, depth extension to d5, 7--8B QLoRA experiments, and all ablations.",
          "+",
          "+\\paragraph{Software.}",
          "+Key library versions: PyTorch 2.x, Transformers 4.x, PEFT 0.x, scikit-learn 1.x.",
          "+Exact versions will be pinned in the released code repository.",
          "+",
          "+\\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}",
          "+LoRA hyperparameters ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, dropout $0.05$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, 3 epochs) follow \\citet{hu2022lora}.",
          "+The Random Forest classifier uses scikit-learn defaults (100 trees, Gini criterion, no max depth) without hyperparameter search; all reported results use 5-fold stratified cross-validation.",
          "+",
          "+\\paragraph{Inference cost.}",
          "+Feature extraction and classification require no GPU.",
          "+Inference takes approximately 2 minutes per 5{,}000 samples on a single CPU core (reference-model forward pass + RF prediction), enabling deployment without specialized hardware."
        ]
      }
    ],

AI Assistant 披露 (Ethics Statement)** ✅\n- 新增：\"This research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts. All scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.\"\n\n**編譯狀態**: 21 頁，無 critical warnings，主文嚴格 ≤8 頁。新增內容均在 Appendix/Ethics Statement，不影響正文頁數。\n\n連同之前的 Final Polish 5 項，全部修改已完成。",

_source line 6366 · 2026-05-18T08:21:06.759Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HsQBb7tfTUCHYQXMm1LbKu`</summary>

```text
38	
39	\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}
40	Reference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\times$ chance.
41	Surprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.
42	For the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.
43	Empirically, the self-model configuration does not yield the highest accuracy (Pythia 78.7\% vs.\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\%), ; the overlap therefore does not confer a systematic advantage.
44	An ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\Delta{\leq}$11\,pp).
45	The choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.
46	Reference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.
47	Simple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\% vs.\ 78.6\%).
48	
49	\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}
50	Independent replication with seed=43 yields 77.15\% (95\% CI [76.4, 78.15], $\Delta{=}{-}1.05$\,pp vs.\ baseline); cross-seed transfer retains 76.76\% ($\Delta{=}{-}1.30$\,pp vs.\ within-seed avg), ; depth signal is thus inherent to the recursive process rather than specific LoRA weights (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_replication}).
51	Top-5 feature importance is identical across seeds.
52	
53	\paragraph{Additional caveats.}
54	Depth signal scales with passage length (68.4\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\% at 512; Appendix~\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.
55	Detection features degrade under domain shift \cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.
56	
57	\section*{Ethics Statement}
```

</details>

    "newString": "85.9\\%); the overlap",
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nCross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.\nPer-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest classifier used throughout the main experiments ($\\Delta < 0.02$\\,pp), and even in the best case (logistic regression) leaves a residual gap relative to within-strategy performance (${\\sim}0.83$); a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is therefore needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-unknown deployment.}\nIn strategy-unknown settings, naive transfer on d1--d3 yields only 45.9\\%, and d1/d2 predictions may invert.\nA two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp over LOO, though per-model gains vary (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\nPractical deployment requires either strategy metadata or learned routing.\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\\% across all nine model--strategy settings), inflating headline accuracy.\nCross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled vs.\\ ecological setting.}\nThe primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination where models train on mixed corpora with prior-generation outputs at unknown proportions.\nDomain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\\%) matches standard CV (72.0\\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain_bias}).\nCross-scale experiments (7--8B, \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show weaker but above-chance signal; a base-model control suggests instruction-tuning regularizes degradation.\nSegment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open; in particular, partial mixing (accumulation), where a single document contains fragments from different recursive depths, is a key open question, as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nDeployment on heterogeneous web corpora requires model- and domain-specific calibration.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nEmpirically, the self-model configuration does not yield the highest accuracy (Pythia 78.7\\% vs.\\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\\%), ; the overlap therefore does not confer a systematic advantage.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nIndependent replication with seed=43 yields 77.15\\% (95\\% CI [76.4, 78.15], $\\Delta{=}{-}1.05$\\,pp vs.\\ baseline); cross-seed transfer retains 76.76\\% ($\\Delta{=}{-}1.30$\\,pp vs.\\ within-seed avg), ; depth signal is thus inherent to the recursive process rather than specific LoRA weights (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).\nTop-5 feature importance is identical across seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\nThis research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.\nAll scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.",

a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is therefore needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-unknown deployment.}\nIn strategy-unknown settings, naive transfer on d1--d3 yields only 45.9\\%, and d1/d2 predictions may invert.\nA two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp over LOO, though per-model gains vary (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\nPractical deployment requires either strategy metadata or learned routing.\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\\% across all nine model--strategy settings), inflating headline accuracy.\nCross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled vs.\\ ecological setting.}\nThe primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination where models train on mixed corpora with prior-generation outputs at unknown proportions.\nDomain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\\%) matches standard CV (72.0\\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain_bias}).\nCross-scale experiments (7--8B, \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show weaker but above-chance signal; a base-model control suggests instruction-tuning regularizes degradation.\nSegment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open; in particular, partial mixing (accumulation), where a single document contains fragments from different recursive depths, is a key open question, as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nDeployment on heterogeneous web corpora requires model- and domain-specific calibration.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nEmpirically, the self-model configuration does not yield the highest accuracy (Pythia 78.7\\% vs.\\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\\%); the overlap therefore does not confer a systematic advantage.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nIndependent replication with seed=43 yields 77.15\\% (95\\% CI [76.4, 78.15], $\\Delta{=}{-}1.05$\\,pp vs.\\ baseline); cross-seed transfer retains 76.76\\% ($\\Delta{=}{-}1.30$\\,pp vs.\\ within-seed avg), ; depth signal is thus inherent to the recursive process rather than specific LoRA weights (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).\nTop-5 feature importance is identical across seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\nThis research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.\nAll scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017AALwQZqKLqSEHt2Mg18Wx`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	The text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.
5	As synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.
6	This model$\to$data$\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical; it is already occurring in the wild. Yet we lack the ability to determine how many recursive iterations a given text has undergone, a distinction that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.
7	
8	Existing tools address adjacent but distinct problems.
9	Binary detectors such as Binoculars \cite{binoculars2024}, Fast-DetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, and DivEye \cite{diveye2026} classify text as human-written or machine-generated, collapsing all depths $d \geq 1$ into a single category.
10	Watermarking \cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding and cannot be applied retroactively.
11	The model collapse literature \cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.
12	No existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone.
13	
14	We find that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are \textbf{consistent in direction but scale-dependent in magnitude} across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.
15	Specifically, perplexity tail statistics (the 90th-percentile perplexity, \textsc{p90\_ppl}, and surprisal variance, \textsc{var\_surprisal}) emerge as dominant depth indicators, ranking in the top two features by importance in 9/9 and 8/9 model--strategy configurations, respectively.
16	Instruction-tuned variants exhibit monotonically decreasing AUC (regularization), whereas base models can show non-monotonic trajectories; instruction tuning thus acts as a regularizer on the depth signal rather than a source of degradation.
17	
18	We formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and introduce a lightweight pipeline: compute 19 reference-model perplexity features (effective dimensionality ${\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \S\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.
19	To characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \times 3$ grid of model families $\times$ decoding strategies spanning 4 depths (180K samples).
20	The atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories and we introduce the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.
```

\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.\nTo characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families $\\times$ decoding strategies spanning 4 depths (180K samples).\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories and we introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization and benchmark.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, introduce the EDD metric, and construct a 180K-sample benchmark spanning 3 model families $\\times$ 3 decoding strategies $\\times$ 4 depths.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} A systematic $3 \\times 3$ characterization reveals that perplexity tail statistics dominate depth discrimination (top-2 in 9/9 and 8/9 cells), decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories qualitatively, and $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for the best-characterized family (Pythia, $p{=}0.001$);           "-Concretely, the d0$\\to$d1 drop in \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} under top-$k$ is 78\\% (vs.\\ 87\\% for nucleus), and the d2$\\to$d3 reduction is 31\\% (vs.\\ 39\\% for nucleus).",

    "oldString": "Extending to 7--8B under QLoRA, Llama-3.1-8B Instruct achieves 70.2\\% ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}4$) and Qwen2.5-7B 60.1\\% ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{>}5$).",
    "newString": "Extending to 7--8B under QLoRA, Llama-3.1-8B Instruct \\cite{llama3} achieves 70.2\\% ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}4$) and Qwen2.5-7B \\cite{qwen2.5} 60.1\\% ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{>}5$).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether atlas accuracy (68.8--85.9\\%) reflects genuine statistical regularities across eight generalization axes (five below, three in Appendix).\n\n\\subsection{Classifier Comparison and Surface-Text Ablation}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} evaluates bagging (RF) and gradient boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM) on the same 19 features; per-cell spread ($\\leq$0.4pp) establishes a feature-determined ceiling.\nOn Pythia nucleus, CORAL \\cite{coral2020} (78.1\\%) and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} (78.2\\%) match RF within 0.6\\,pp ($p>0.05$); all feature-based methods outperform CE-MLP (74.9\\%) and logistic regression (75.0\\%).\nBinary detectors repurposed for ordinal classification (Binoculars + Fast-DetectGPT combined: 74.2\\%) fall 4\\,pp below RF (Appendix~\\ref{app:detector_baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{Surface-text ablation.}\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%); zero-shot LLMs (DeepSeek V3.2, 36.2\\%; Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, 30.4\\%) also fail (43\\,pp below RF; Appendix~\\ref{app:zeroshot_prompt}); depth signal therefore resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Classifier convergence: 4-class accuracy (\\%) across three atlas cells. RF (bagging), XGBoost and LightGBM (gradient boosting) operate on the same 19 features; $\\Delta_{\\max}$: largest per-cell spread. Chance = 25\\%. RF values differ from Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} by $\\leq$0.2pp due to matched-fold evaluation. 5-fold cross-validation standard deviation $\\leq$ 0.9\\,pp across all settings.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & RF & XGB & LGBM & $\\Delta_{\\max}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc. & 78.8 & 79.0 & 78.9 & 0.2 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, $\\tau$ & 85.8 & 86.2 & 86.2 & 0.4 \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$ & 68.9 & 68.9 & 68.7 & 0.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nConvergence confirms the signal resides in the features; three alternative classifiers match RF within 0.8\\,pp under cross-model transfer (Appendix~\\ref{app:learned_estimator}).\nPerplexity features alone recover 66.0\\% ($-$0.5\\,pp vs.\\ full), while repetition/diversity reach only 53.0\\% ($-$13.6\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_ablation}); perplexity degradation is the dominant depth marker.\nRaw perplexity alone performs at chance (25.1\\%); the strongest single feature (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) reaches 58.3\\% ($-$8.3\\,pp vs.\\ full); multi-dimensional characterization is required.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 near-perfectly identified; errors concentrate at d2/d3 in-distribution and spread to d1--d3 under transfer (seed std $= 0.06$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_seed}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.9\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d1--d3 bal.\\ acc: Pythia 49.9\\%, OLMo 59.2\\%, GPT-2~XL 57.2\\% (mean 55.4\\%).}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy is 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance); d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline (d1--d3 balanced mean 55.4\\%).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy} reports leave-one-strategy-out transfer.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d0 recall $\\geq$89\\% inflates headline; d1--d3 mean: 45.9\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7pp below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%); excluding d0, d1--d3 accuracy drops to 45.9\\% (chance = 33\\%).\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ inverts the d1/d2 boundary under temperature (d1v2 AUC $<$ 0.5); a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + depth classification) recovers 11.5\\,pp over naive transfer (Limitations).\nA multi-temperature ablation (Appendix~\\ref{app:multi_tau}) confirms within-$\\tau$ signal for $\\tau \\leq 0.9$ but collapse at $\\tau{=}1.1$; a 1.5B instruct control (47.4\\%, $1.9\\times$ chance) confirms at matched scale (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct_1p5b}).\nCross-model chains (84.75\\%), cross-domain (arXiv 74.1\\%), and LoRA rank sensitivity ($\\Delta{=}4.65$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) further confirm robustness.",

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nCross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.\nPer-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest classifier used throughout the main experiments ($\\Delta < 0.02$\\,pp), and even in the best case (logistic regression) leaves a residual gap relative to within-strategy performance (${\\sim}0.83$); a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is therefore needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-unknown deployment.}\nIn strategy-unknown settings, naive transfer on d1--d3 yields only 45.9\\%, and d1/d2 predictions may invert.\nA two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp over LOO, though per-model gains vary (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\nPractical deployment requires either strategy metadata or learned routing.\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\\% across all nine model--strategy settings), inflating headline accuracy.\nCross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled vs.\\ ecological setting.}\nThe primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination where models train on mixed corpora with prior-generation outputs at unknown proportions.\nDomain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\\%) matches standard CV (72.0\\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain_bias}).\nCross-scale experiments (7--8B, \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show weaker but above-chance signal; a base-model control suggests instruction-tuning regularizes degradation.\nSegment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open; in particular, partial mixing (accumulation), where a single document contains fragments from different recursive depths, is a key open question, as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nDeployment on heterogeneous web corpora requires model- and domain-specific calibration.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nEmpirically, the self-model configuration does not yield the highest accuracy (Pythia 78.7\\% vs.\\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\\%); the overlap therefore does not confer a systematic advantage.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nIndependent replication with seed=43 yields 77.15\\% (95\\% CI [76.4, 78.15], $\\Delta{=}{-}1.05$\\,pp vs.\\ baseline); cross-seed transfer retains 76.76\\% ($\\Delta{=}{-}1.30$\\,pp vs.\\ within-seed avg); depth signal is thus inherent to the recursive process rather than specific LoRA weights (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).\nTop-5 feature importance is identical across seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\nThis research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.\nAll scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "tulchinskii2024_intrinsic_dim",
    "newString": "tulchinskii2023_intrinsic_dim",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Model Collapse Theory.}\n\\citet{shumailov2024_nature} demonstrate that recursive training on model outputs erodes distributional tails; \\citet{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} formalize this via scaling laws \\cite{seddik2024synthetic}, \\citet{dohmatob2025_strong_collapse} establish strong collapse guarantees, and the MAD framework \\cite{alemohammad2024_mad} extends the analysis to self-consuming models.\nSubsequent work addresses mitigation \\cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate,drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse,yi2026_escaping_collapse,falahati2026_curated,wenzek2020_ccnet}, non-monotonic dynamics \\cite{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement}, convergence \\cite{marchi2024_heat_death,wang2026_contamination,knowledge_collapse2025,dohmatob2024_demystified}, and diversity decline \\cite{guo2024curious,briesch2024selfconsuming,padmakumar2024diversity,sigma2026_spectral,synthetic_eggs2026}.\n\\citet{schaeffer2025} argue that ``model collapse'' conflates multiple distinct phenomena, and call for operationally precise metrics.\nDohmatob et al.\\ predict that recursive training erodes distributional tails as a theoretical scaling law \\citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; we operationalize this prediction into a deployable ordinal classifier, revealing strategy-dependent trajectories and non-monotonic 7B behavior that the theory does not predict.\nAll assume depth is known a priori; we address the inverse problem of estimating depth from text alone.\n\n\\paragraph{AI Text Detection.}\nThe detection literature \\cite{wu2025_detection_survey} spans statistical methods (DetectGPT \\cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt}, Fast-DetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, GLTR \\cite{gehrmann2019_gltr}, Ghostbuster \\cite{verma2024_ghostbuster}, DNA-GPT \\cite{yang2024_dnagpt}), geometric approaches based on intrinsic dimensionality \\cite{tulchinskii2024_intrinsic_dim}, and watermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark}, with known limits from paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase}, distribution shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and generator barriers \\cite{pu2026_generator_barrier}.\nM4-SemEval \\cite{wang2024_m4_semeval}, RAID \\cite{dugan2024_raid}, and MAGE \\cite{li2024_mage} benchmark detection across domains, models, and adversarial conditions.\nAll these methods perform binary classification, discarding ordinal depth information; our approach outputs ordinal depth labels, complementing rather than replacing binary detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Adjacent Tasks.}\nEditLens \\cite{editlens2026} estimates editing extent but does not model recursive chains; \\citet{uchendu2020_attribution} classify \\emph{which} model generated text; LOKI \\cite{loki2025} benchmarks synthetic data detection across modalities.\n\\citet{mohamed2025_broken_telephone} study iterative generation as a ``broken telephone'' effect, characterizing \\emph{how} text degrades without classifying which iteration a passage comes from.\nOur task performs the inverse, ordinal depth estimation from text alone, discriminating between adjacent depths rather than merely detecting aggregate degradation.\n\n\\paragraph{Ordinal Classification.}\nOrdinal classification has a long history \\cite{mccullagh1980_ordinal}: \\citet{frank2001} reduce ordinal problems to nested binary tasks, and \\citet{rennie2005} introduce loss functions tailored to discrete ordered labels.\nCORAL \\cite{coral2020} enforces rank consistency via ordered binary tasks; CLOC \\cite{cloc2025} extends contrastive learning with multi-margin ordinal losses.\nWe adopt both as baselines (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}) to confirm the signal is accessible to principled ordinal methods.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding Strategy Effects.}\nDecoding strategies shape text statistics in ways that interact with depth signals \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus,finlayson2024_closing,garces_arias2025_decoding_decoded,wiher2022_decoding,basu2021_mirostat}.\nOur Depth Degradation Atlas (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}) reveals that these strategies induce qualitatively different degradation trajectories under recursive generation: temperature sampling accelerates convergence to a low-entropy attractor while top-$k$ attenuates the depth signal, effects beyond single-pass text quality.",

          "+The detection literature \\cite{wu2025_detection_survey} spans statistical methods (DetectGPT \\cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt}, Fast-DetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, GLTR \\cite{gehrmann2019_gltr}, Ghostbuster \\cite{verma2024_ghostbuster}, DNA-GPT \\cite{yang2024_dnagpt}), geometric approaches based on intrinsic dimensionality \\cite{tulchinskii2023_intrinsic_dim}, and watermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark}, with known limits from paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase}, distribution shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and generator barriers \\cite{pu2026_generator_barrier}.",
          " M4-SemEval \\cite{wang2024_m4_semeval}, RAID \\cite{dugan2024_raid}, and MAGE \\cite{li2024_mage} benchmark detection across domains, models, and adversarial conditions.",

     LoRA fine-tuning loss decreases monotonically with depth for all three families, a direct empirical validation of the tail erosion predicted by \citet{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; instruction tuning regularizes this compression (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}).

        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Model Collapse Theory.}\n\\citet{shumailov2024_nature} demonstrate that recursive training on model outputs erodes distributional tails; \\citet{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} formalize this via scaling laws \\cite{seddik2024synthetic}, \\citet{dohmatob2025_strong_collapse} establish strong collapse guarantees, and the MAD framework \\cite{alemohammad2024_mad} extends the analysis to self-consuming models.\nSubsequent work addresses mitigation \\cite{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate,drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse,yi2026_escaping_collapse,falahati2026_curated,wenzek2020_ccnet}, non-monotonic dynamics \\cite{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement}, convergence \\cite{marchi2024_heat_death,wang2026_contamination,knowledge_collapse2025,dohmatob2024_demystified}, and diversity decline \\cite{guo2024curious,briesch2024selfconsuming,padmakumar2024diversity,sigma2026_spectral,synthetic_eggs2026}.\n\\citet{schaeffer2025} argue that ``model collapse'' conflates multiple distinct phenomena, and call for operationally precise metrics.\nDohmatob et al.\\ predict that recursive training erodes distributional tails as a theoretical scaling law \\citep{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; we operationalize this prediction into a deployable ordinal classifier, revealing strategy-dependent trajectories and non-monotonic 7B behavior that the theory does not predict.\nAll assume depth is known a priori; we address the inverse problem of estimating depth from text alone.\n\n\\paragraph{AI Text Detection.}\nThe detection literature \\cite{wu2025_detection_survey} spans statistical methods (DetectGPT \\cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt}, Fast-DetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, GLTR \\cite{gehrmann2019_gltr}, Ghostbuster \\cite{verma2024_ghostbuster}, DNA-GPT \\cite{yang2024_dnagpt}), geometric approaches based on intrinsic dimensionality \\cite{tulchinskii2023_intrinsic_dim}, and watermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark}, with known limits from paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase}, distribution shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and generator barriers \\cite{pu2026_generator_barrier}.\nM4-SemEval \\cite{wang2024_m4_semeval}, RAID \\cite{dugan2024_raid}, and MAGE \\cite{li2024_mage} benchmark detection across domains, models, and adversarial conditions.\nAll these methods perform binary classification, discarding ordinal depth information; our approach outputs ordinal depth labels, complementing rather than replacing binary detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Adjacent Tasks.}\nEditLens \\cite{editlens2026} estimates editing extent but does not model recursive chains; \\citet{uchendu2020_attribution} classify \\emph{which} model generated text; LOKI \\cite{loki2025} benchmarks synthetic data detection across modalities.\n\\citet{mohamed2025_broken_telephone} study iterative generation as a ``broken telephone'' effect, characterizing \\emph{how} text degrades without classifying which iteration a passage comes from.\nOur task performs the inverse, ordinal depth estimation from text alone, discriminating between adjacent depths rather than merely detecting aggregate degradation.\n\n\\paragraph{Ordinal Classification.}\nOrdinal classification has a long history \\cite{mccullagh1980_ordinal}: \\citet{frank2001} reduce ordinal problems to nested binary tasks, and \\citet{rennie2005} introduce loss functions tailored to discrete ordered labels.\nCORAL \\cite{coral2020} enforces rank consistency via ordered binary tasks; CLOC \\cite{cloc2025} extends contrastive learning with multi-margin ordinal losses.\nWe adopt both as baselines (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}) to confirm the signal is accessible to principled ordinal methods.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding Strategy Effects.}\nDecoding strategies shape text statistics in ways that interact with depth signals \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus,finlayson2024_closing,garces_arias2025_decoding_decoded,wiher2022_decoding,basu2021_mirostat}.\nOur Depth Degradation Atlas (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}) reveals that these strategies induce qualitatively different degradation trajectories under recursive generation: temperature sampling accelerates convergence to a low-entropy attractor while top-$k$ attenuates the depth signal, effects beyond single-pass text quality.",

        "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nCross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.\nPer-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest classifier used throughout the main experiments ($\\Delta < 0.02$\\,pp), and even in the best case (logistic regression) leaves a residual gap relative to within-strategy performance (${\\sim}0.83$); a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is therefore needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-unknown deployment.}\nIn strategy-unknown settings, naive transfer on d1--d3 yields only 45.9\\%, and d1/d2 predictions may invert.\nA two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp over LOO, though per-model gains vary (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\nPractical deployment requires either strategy metadata or learned routing.\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\\% across all nine model--strategy settings), inflating headline accuracy.\nCross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled vs.\\ ecological setting.}\nThe primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination where models train on mixed corpora with prior-generation outputs at unknown proportions.\nDomain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\\%) matches standard CV (72.0\\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain_bias}).\nCross-scale experiments (7--8B, \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show weaker but above-chance signal; a base-model control suggests instruction-tuning regularizes degradation.\nSegment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open; in particular, partial mixing (accumulation), where a single document contains fragments from different recursive depths, is a key open question, as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb \\cite{penedo2024_fineweb}; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nDeployment on heterogeneous web corpora requires model- and domain-specific calibration.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nEmpirically, the self-model configuration does not yield the highest accuracy (Pythia 78.7\\% vs.\\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\\%); the overlap therefore does not confer a systematic advantage.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nIndependent replication with seed=43 yields 77.15\\% (95\\% CI [76.4, 78.15], $\\Delta{=}{-}1.05$\\,pp vs.\\ baseline); cross-seed transfer retains 76.76\\% ($\\Delta{=}{-}1.30$\\,pp vs.\\ within-seed avg); depth signal is thus inherent to the recursive process rather than specific LoRA weights (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).\nTop-5 feature importance is identical across seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\nThis research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.\nAll scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.",
        "numLines": 64,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 64
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/conclusion.tex"
  },
