        "content": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether atlas accuracy (68.8--85.9\\%) reflects genuine statistical regularities across eight generalization axes (five below, three in Appendix).\n\n\\subsection{Classifier Comparison and Surface-Text Ablation}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} evaluates bagging (RF) and gradient boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM) on the same 19 features; per-cell spread ($\\leq$0.4pp) establishes a feature-determined ceiling.\nOn Pythia nucleus, CORAL \\cite{coral2020} (78.1\\%) and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} (78.2\\%) match RF within 0.6\\,pp ($p>0.05$); all feature-based methods outperform CE-MLP (74.9\\%) and logistic regression (75.0\\%).\nBinary detectors repurposed for ordinal classification (Binoculars + Fast-DetectGPT combined: 74.2\\%) fall 4\\,pp below RF (Appendix~\\ref{app:detector_baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{Surface-text ablation.}\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%); zero-shot LLMs (DeepSeek V3.2, 36.2\\%; Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, 30.4\\%) also fail (43\\,pp below RF; Appendix~\\ref{app:zeroshot_prompt}); depth signal therefore resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Classifier convergence: 4-class accuracy (\\%) across three atlas cells. RF (bagging), XGBoost and LightGBM (gradient boosting) operate on the same 19 features; $\\Delta_{\\max}$: largest per-cell spread. Chance = 25\\%. RF values differ from Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} by $\\leq$0.2pp due to matched-fold evaluation. 5-fold cross-validation standard deviation $\\leq$ 0.9\\,pp across all settings.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & RF & XGB & LGBM & $\\Delta_{\\max}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc. & 78.8 & 79.0 & 78.9 & 0.2 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, $\\tau$ & 85.8 & 86.2 & 86.2 & 0.4 \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$ & 68.9 & 68.9 & 68.7 & 0.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nConvergence confirms the signal resides in the features; three alternative classifiers match RF within 0.8\\,pp under cross-model transfer (Appendix~\\ref{app:learned_estimator}).\nPerplexity features alone recover 66.0\\% ($-$0.5\\,pp vs.\\ full), while repetition/diversity reach only 53.0\\% ($-$13.6\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_ablation}); perplexity degradation is the dominant depth marker.\nRaw perplexity alone performs at chance (25.1\\%); the strongest single feature (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) reaches 58.3\\% ($-$8.3\\,pp vs.\\ full); multi-dimensional characterization is required.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 near-perfectly identified; errors concentrate at d2/d3 in-distribution and spread to d1--d3 under transfer (seed std $= 0.06$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_seed}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.9\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d1--d3 bal.\\ acc: Pythia 49.9\\%, OLMo 59.2\\%, GPT-2~XL 57.2\\% (mean 55.4\\%).}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy is 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance); d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline (d1--d3 balanced mean 55.4\\%).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy} reports leave-one-strategy-out transfer.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d0 recall $\\geq$89\\% inflates headline; d1--d3 mean: 45.9\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7pp below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%); excluding d0, d1--d3 accuracy drops to 45.9\\% (chance = 33\\%).\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ inverts the d1/d2 boundary under temperature (d1v2 AUC $<$ 0.5); a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + depth classification) recovers 11.5\\,pp over naive transfer (Limitations).\nA multi-temperature ablation (Appendix~\\ref{app:multi_tau}) confirms within-$\\tau$ signal for $\\tau \\leq 0.9$ but collapse at $\\tau{=}1.1$; end-to-end PLMs lag RF by 20pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:e2e}).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompted generation.}\nReplicating the GPT-2~XL nucleus pipeline with topic-seeded generation yields 69.5\\% (95\\% CI $[68.9, 70.2]$, $-$13.8\\,pp vs.\\ BOS-seeded, $2.8\\times$ chance), with monotonic AUC decay and identical top features (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompted_gen}): prompt conditioning attenuates but does not eliminate the depth signal.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Preliminary Cross-Scale Observations}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossscale}\n\nExtending to 7--8B under QLoRA, Llama-3.1-8B Instruct \\cite{llama3} achieves 70.2\\% ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}4$) and Qwen2.5-7B \\cite{qwen2.5} 60.1\\% ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{>}5$).\nA base-model control (Llama-3.1-8B, 59.9\\%, non-monotonic AUC) vs.\\ instruction-tuned (70.2\\%, monotonic) suggests instruction tuning \\emph{regularizes} recursive degradation; a 1.5B instruct control (47.4\\%, $1.9\\times$ chance) confirms at matched scale (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct_1p5b}).\nCross-model chains (84.75\\%), cross-domain (arXiv 74.1\\%), and LoRA rank sensitivity ($\\Delta{=}4.65$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) further confirm robustness.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EsjumMGzsqU8JEyD1mXBJR`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Problem Definition}
5	\label{sec:method:problem}
6	
7	Let $f_\theta$ denote a language model and $\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.
8	We define a \emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\mathcal{D}_0, \mathcal{D}_1, \dots, \mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\theta$ on $\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.
9	Depth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.
10	
11	Given a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \in \{0, 1, \dots, K\}$.
12	This is an ordinal classification problem: depths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.
13	
14	\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}
15	\label{sec:method:benchmark}
16	
17	We construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.
18	
19	\paragraph{Model families.}
20	We select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).
21	Restricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.
22	
23	\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}
24	Each model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).
25	These span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.
26	Our findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.
27	
28	\paragraph{Depth and scale.}
29	The primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).
30	For depth extension experiments (\S\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.
31	
32	\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}
33	Starting from a Wikipedia subset as seed text ($\mathcal{D}_0$), each generation cycle proceeds in three steps: (1)~LoRA finetuning \cite{hu2022lora} on $\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$, freezing base model weights and training low-rank update matrices ($<$1\% additional parameters; $r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$, architecture-specific targets); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages from the LoRA-finetuned model (max 512 tokens, BOS-seeded, no prompt conditioning) under the specified decoding strategy; (3)~the generated corpus becomes $\mathcal{D}_k$ for the next cycle.
34	Each depth is produced by a model explicitly trained on the prior depth's data, not by sampling from the unmodified base model.
35	Our BOS-seeded generation may amplify depth signal relative to prompted settings; a prompted-generation validation (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) confirms signal persistence under topic-seeded conditions ($-$13.8\,pp vs.\ BOS-seeded).
36	There is no per-passage lineage across depths; cross-validation splits therefore do not introduce information leakage.
37	
38	\paragraph{Reference model.}
39	All statistical features (\S\ref{sec:method:features}) are computed using Pythia-1.4B as fixed reference model, the smallest generator whose training corpus partially overlaps with the others.
40	For Pythia this constitutes self-model measurement, yet its accuracy (78.7\%) does not exceed GPT-2~XL (85.9\%); no systematic self-model advantage is observed.
41	Ablations with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references yield 67--79\% accuracy (Appendix~\ref{app:ref_ablation}); reference model robustness is confirmed. Further discussion in Limitations.
42	
43	\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}
44	\label{sec:method:features}
45	
46	We extract a feature vector $\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\S\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\% of full-set performance, and variance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features; effective independent dimensionality is ${\approx}$4--5.
47	The features fall into four categories (Table~\ref{tab:features}).
48	
49	\begin{table}[t]
50	\centering
51	\small
52	\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (Pythia-1.4B).}
53	\label{tab:features}
54	\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}
55	\toprule
56	Category & Features & $n$ \\
57	\midrule
58	Perplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\
59	statistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\
60	\addlinespace
61	Surprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\
62	statistics & & \\
63	\addlinespace
64	Lexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\
65	diversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\
66	\addlinespace
67	Repetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\
68	\bottomrule
69	\end{tabular}
70	\end{table}
71	
72	\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments (mean, variance, skewness, kurtosis) and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles prove especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\S\ref{sec:atlas}).
73	\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\log p(\text{token})$; surprisal variance captures second-order structure not redundant with perplexity moments.
74	\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.
75	\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \in \{2, 3, 4\}$.
76	
77	\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}
78	\label{sec:method:edd}
79	
80	Aggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.
81	We propose the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) as an operational measure of how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.
82	
83	\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}
84	Let $\mathcal{D} = \{0, 1, \dots, K\}$ denote the depth set and $T \in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.
85	For each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\lfloor\sqrt{19}\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\S\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.
86	Each pair uses an independent classifier, avoiding the decision boundary dilution that arises in multi-class settings (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
87	
88	\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]
89	\label{def:edd}
90	The \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:
91	\begin{equation}
92	\mathrm{EDD}_T = \max \bigl\{ d^*{+}1 \,:\, \forall\, d' \leq d^*,\; \mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \geq T \bigr\}
93	\label{eq:edd}
94	\end{equation}
95	where $d^* \in \{0, \dots, K{-}1\}$. If\, $\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.
96	\end{definition}
97	
98	$\mathrm{EDD}_T$ is the deepest depth $d$ such that every adjacent pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ remains discriminable at level $T$.
99	The universal quantifier ensures EDD reports the longest \emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix rather than an isolated high-AUC pair at greater depth.\footnote{Our AUC profiles are empirically monotonically decreasing for the 1--1.5B base models (\S\ref{sec:atlas}), so in practice $\mathrm{EDD}_T = \max\{d'{+}1 : \mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \geq T\}$. Qwen2.5-7B exhibits a U-shaped AUC pattern rather than monotonic decay; a base-model control shows non-monotonic decay persists without instruction tuning (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}). For non-monotonic profiles, the contiguous formulation is more conservative (see Limitations).}
100	We set $T{=}0.75$ as default; sensitivity at $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$ is reported in \S\ref{sec:atlas}.
101	
102	\paragraph{Classifiers.}
103	Our primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold stratified cross-validation).
104	We compare against CORAL ordinal regression \cite{coral2020}, DivEye \cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \S\ref{sec:generalization}.
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	We formalized recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and showed that perplexity tail statistics achieve 69--86\% four-class accuracy ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$), with five features sufficing for 97.6\% of full performance.
5	LoRA fine-tuning loss decreases monotonically with depth for all three families, a direct empirical validation of the tail erosion predicted by \citet{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; instruction tuning regularizes this compression (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}).
6	The non-uniform AUC contraction across adjacent pairs (ratio 0.69--0.80) is qualitatively consistent with exponential decay but deviates quantitatively.
7	RF-filtered subsets outperform random subsampling ($p{<}0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:curation_poc}), and cross-model chains (84.75\%; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_boundary}) confirm the signal is process-level; cross-strategy transfer and depth-quality entanglement remain open challenges (Limitations; Appendix~\ref{app:extended_discussion}).
8	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017KRD1tpYjoe5gdZu5LFLmp`</summary>

```text
215-\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}
216:\label{app:calibration}
217-
218-Table~\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages, crawled 2019--2020; pre-LLM deployment).
219-
--
392-\paragraph{Confounds.}
393:\label{app:cross_scale_confounds}
394-Four confounds limit these results: (1)~4-bit NF4 QLoRA quantization (absent at 1--1.5B) may distort the perplexity distribution independently of depth; (2)~smaller sample size (500 vs.\ 5{,}000 per depth) widens confidence intervals; (3)~evaluation is limited to nucleus sampling, leaving other strategies untested at this scale; (4)~vocabulary mismatch between 7--8B generators and the 1.4B reference model.
395-The base-model control partially addresses a former confound (instruction-tuning): since the base variant shows weaker signal with non-monotonic decay while the instruction-tuned variant shows stronger monotonic signal, instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds depth estimation.
396-The remaining confounds are not mutually independent (quantization and vocabulary mismatch both affect the perplexity distribution); it is therefore infeasible to attribute the observed accuracy gap (60--70\% vs.\ 78--85\% at 1--1.5B) to any single factor.
--
484-\subsection{Mixed-Depth Corpus}
485:\label{app:mixed_depth}
486-
487-Per-passage classification is largely independent of corpus composition: evaluating on a corpus containing mixed proportions of d0--d3 passages (rather than balanced classes) yields macro-recall $\Delta{=}{-}1.62$\,pp relative to the balanced benchmark (within the 2\,pp tolerance).
488-Individual class deltas range from $-$0.45\,pp to $-$2.43\,pp (d1 shows the largest shift); the RF classifier operates on per-sample features without sensitivity to the class distribution of the evaluation corpus.
--
530-\subsection{Downstream Fine-Tuning Probe}
531:\label{app:downstream}
532-
533-We fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$) on depth-stratified subsets (500 samples each) and evaluate held-out perplexity (200 samples, 50 per depth).
534-
--
668-\subsection{Computational Resources and Reproducibility}
669:\label{app:compute}
670-
671-\paragraph{Hardware.}
672-All experiments were conducted on 2$\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\,GB) GPUs locally and 2$\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\,GB) on cloud instances.
```

</details>

    "content": "215-\\subsection{Wild-Data Calibration}\n216:\\label{app:calibration}\n217-\n218-Table~\\ref{tab:calibration} reports d0 accuracy and false positive rate (FPR) for all three model-specific nucleus RF classifiers on two out-of-distribution human-text corpora: WikiText-103 (546 editorial passages) and C4 (5{,}000 web-crawl passages, crawled 2019--2020; pre-LLM deployment).\n219-\n--\n392-\\paragraph{Confounds.}\n393:\\label{app:cross_scale_confounds}\n394-Four confounds limit these results: (1)~4-bit NF4 QLoRA quantization (absent at 1--1.5B) may distort the perplexity distribution independently of depth; (2)~smaller sample size (500 vs.\\ 5{,}000 per depth) widens confidence intervals; (3)~evaluation is limited to nucleus sampling, leaving other strategies untested at this scale; (4)~vocabulary mismatch between 7--8B generators and the 1.4B reference model.\n395-The base-model control partially addresses a former confound (instruction-tuning): since the base variant shows weaker signal with non-monotonic decay while the instruction-tuned variant shows stronger monotonic signal, instruction-tuning regularizes rather than confounds depth estimation.\n396-The remaining confounds are not mutually independent (quantization and vocabulary mismatch both affect the perplexity distribution); it is therefore infeasible to attribute the observed accuracy gap (60--70\\% vs.\\ 78--85\\% at 1--1.5B) to any single factor.\n--\n484-\\subsection{Mixed-Depth Corpus}\n485:\\label{app:mixed_depth}\n486-\n487-Per-passage classification is largely independent of corpus composition: evaluating on a corpus containing mixed proportions of d0--d3 passages (rather than balanced classes) yields macro-recall $\\Delta{=}{-}1.62$\\,pp relative to the balanced benchmark (within the 2\\,pp tolerance).\n488-Individual class deltas range from $-$0.45\\,pp to $-$2.43\\,pp (d1 shows the largest shift); the RF classifier operates on per-sample features without sensitivity to the class distribution of the evaluation corpus.\n--\n530-\\subsection{Downstream Fine-Tuning Probe}\n531:\\label{app:downstream}\n532-\n533-We fine-tune Pythia-410M with LoRA ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$) on depth-stratified subsets (500 samples each) and evaluate held-out perplexity (200 samples, 50 per depth).\n534-\n--\n668-\\subsection{Computational Resources and Reproducibility}\n669:\\label{app:compute}\n670-\n671-\\paragraph{Hardware.}\n672-All experiments were conducted on 2$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\\,GB) GPUs locally and 2$\\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 (48\\,GB) on cloud instances.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017v4p62jLwNrMHBEagL6VyX`</summary>

```text
0	\section*{Limitations}
1	\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}
4	Cross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.
5	Per-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest classifier used throughout the main experiments ($\Delta < 0.02$\,pp), and even in the best case (logistic regression) leaves a residual gap relative to within-strategy performance (${\sim}0.83$); a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is therefore needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.
6	
7	\paragraph{Strategy-unknown deployment.}
8	In strategy-unknown settings, naive transfer on d1--d3 yields only 45.9\%, and d1/d2 predictions may invert.
9	A two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\,pp over LOO, though per-model gains vary (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\,pp vs.\ Pythia $+$8.0\,pp).
10	Practical deployment requires either strategy metadata or learned routing.
11	
12	\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}
13	Depth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\% across all nine model--strategy settings), inflating headline accuracy.
14	Cross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).
15	
16	\paragraph{Controlled vs.\ ecological setting.}
17	The primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination where models train on mixed corpora with prior-generation outputs at unknown proportions.
18	Domain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\%) matches standard CV (72.0\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\%; Appendix~\ref{app:domain_bias}).
19	Cross-scale experiments (7--8B, \S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show weaker but above-chance signal; a base-model control suggests instruction-tuning regularizes degradation.
20	Segment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open; in particular, partial mixing (accumulation), where a single document contains fragments from different recursive depths, is a key open question, as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.
21	
22	\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}
23	On editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\geq$98.5\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\% (OLMo) to 22.4\% (GPT-2~XL).
24	A pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\% FPR on C4 and 17\% on FineWeb \cite{penedo2024_fineweb}; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.
25	Misclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\texttt{rep\_3gram} $+$37\%, \texttt{rep\_4gram} $+$75\% vs.\ correctly classified d0).
26	Deployment on heterogeneous web corpora requires model- and domain-specific calibration.
27	
28	\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}
29	Adversarial paraphrasing \cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.
30	
31	\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}
32	Our EDD definition (Eq.~\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.
33	However, \citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).
34	Our cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.
35	In such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.
36	The choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.
37	
38	\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}
39	Reference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\times$ chance.
40	Surprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.
41	For the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.
42	Empirically, the self-model configuration does not yield the highest accuracy (Pythia 78.7\% vs.\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\%); the overlap therefore does not confer a systematic advantage.
43	An ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\Delta{\leq}$11\,pp).
44	The choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.
45	Reference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.
46	Simple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\% vs.\ 78.6\%).
47	
48	\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}
49	Independent replication with seed=43 yields 77.15\% (95\% CI [76.4, 78.15], $\Delta{=}{-}1.05$\,pp vs.\ baseline); cross-seed transfer retains 76.76\% ($\Delta{=}{-}1.30$\,pp vs.\ within-seed avg); depth signal is thus inherent to the recursive process rather than specific LoRA weights (Appendix~\ref{app:seed_replication}).
50	Top-5 feature importance is identical across seeds.
51	
52	\paragraph{Additional caveats.}
53	Depth signal scales with passage length (68.4\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\% at 512; Appendix~\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.
54	Detection features degrade under domain shift \cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.
55	
56	\section*{Ethics Statement}
57	
58	This work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.
59	No human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.
60	Our method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.
61	Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
62	This research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.
63	All scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.
```

Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\nThis research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.\nAll scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.",

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nCross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.\nPer-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest classifier used throughout the main experiments ($\\Delta < 0.02$\\,pp), and even in the best case (logistic regression) leaves a residual gap relative to within-strategy performance (${\\sim}0.83$); a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is therefore needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-unknown deployment.}\nIn strategy-unknown settings, naive transfer on d1--d3 yields only 45.9\\%, and d1/d2 predictions may invert.\nA two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp over LOO, though per-model gains vary (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\nPractical deployment requires either strategy metadata or learned routing.\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\\% across all nine model--strategy settings), inflating headline accuracy.\nCross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled vs.\\ ecological setting.}\nThe primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination where models train on mixed corpora with prior-generation outputs at unknown proportions.\nDomain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\\%) matches standard CV (72.0\\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain_bias}).\nCross-scale experiments (7--8B, \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show weaker but above-chance signal; a base-model control suggests instruction-tuning regularizes degradation.\nSegment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open; in particular, partial mixing (accumulation), where a single document contains fragments from different recursive depths, is a key open question, as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb \\cite{penedo2024_fineweb}; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nDeployment on heterogeneous web corpora requires model- and domain-specific calibration.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nEmpirically, the self-model configuration does not yield the highest accuracy (Pythia 78.7\\% vs.\\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\\%); the overlap therefore does not confer a systematic advantage.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nIndependent replication with seed=43 yields 77.15\\% (95\\% CI [76.4, 78.15], $\\Delta{=}{-}1.05$\\,pp vs.\\ baseline); cross-seed transfer retains 76.76\\% ($\\Delta{=}{-}1.30$\\,pp vs.\\ within-seed avg); depth signal is thus inherent to the recursive process rather than specific LoRA weights (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).\nTop-5 feature importance is identical across seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\nThis research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.\nAll scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "31e84e95-6e4e-47fe-8a0f-ca9eed8225db",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "3c232dd6-fb2f-4a29-9b62-cddff2828f46",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "41a0e232-890e-4b25-b8e2-3399a1010911",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "We compare against CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",
    "newString": "We compare against CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.\nFull computational details (hardware, GPU hours, software versions) are in Appendix~\\ref{app:compute}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nThis is an ordinal classification problem: depths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\nOur findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset as seed text ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each generation cycle proceeds in three steps: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$, freezing base model weights and training low-rank update matrices ($<$1\\% additional parameters; $r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, batch size 8, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$, architecture-specific targets); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages from the LoRA-finetuned model (max 512 tokens, BOS-seeded, no prompt conditioning) under the specified decoding strategy; (3)~the generated corpus becomes $\\mathcal{D}_k$ for the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model explicitly trained on the prior depth's data, not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nOur BOS-seeded generation may amplify depth signal relative to prompted settings; a prompted-generation validation (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) confirms signal persistence under topic-seeded conditions ($-$13.8\\,pp vs.\\ BOS-seeded).\nThere is no per-passage lineage across depths; cross-validation splits therefore do not introduce information leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) are computed using Pythia-1.4B as fixed reference model, the smallest generator whose training corpus partially overlaps with the others.\nFor Pythia this constitutes self-model measurement, yet its accuracy (78.7\\%) does not exceed GPT-2~XL (85.9\\%); no systematic self-model advantage is observed.\nAblations with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references yield 67--79\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}); reference model robustness is confirmed. Further discussion in Limitations.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\\% of full-set performance, and variance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features; effective independent dimensionality is ${\\approx}$4--5.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (Pythia-1.4B).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments (mean, variance, skewness, kurtosis) and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles prove especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$; surprisal variance captures second-order structure not redundant with perplexity moments.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe propose the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) as an operational measure of how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\nEach pair uses an independent classifier, avoiding the decision boundary dilution that arises in multi-class settings (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$. If\\, $\\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.\n\\end{definition}\n\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ is the deepest depth $d$ such that every adjacent pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ remains discriminable at level $T$.\nThe universal quantifier ensures EDD reports the longest \\emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix rather than an isolated high-AUC pair at greater depth.\\footnote{Our AUC profiles are empirically monotonically decreasing for the 1--1.5B base models (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), so in practice $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max\\{d'{+}1 : \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T\\}$. Qwen2.5-7B exhibits a U-shaped AUC pattern rather than monotonic decay; a base-model control shows non-monotonic decay persists without instruction tuning (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}). For non-monotonic profiles, the contiguous formulation is more conservative (see Limitations).}\nWe set $T{=}0.75$ as default; sensitivity at $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$ is reported in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas}.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold stratified cross-validation).\nWe compare against CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, a 2-layer cross-entropy MLP, logistic regression, and a single-feature baseline using mean perplexity alone; results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",

    "newString": "Segment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open (Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_depth}); in particular, partial mixing (accumulation), where a single document contains fragments from different recursive depths, is a key open question, as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.",
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-strategy deployment constraint.}\nCross-strategy transfer induces a d1/d2 boundary inversion under temperature sampling.\nPer-strategy z-score normalization partially corrects the inversion under logistic regression (GPT-2~XL d1v2 AUC $0.24{\\to}0.56$, OLMo $0.19{\\to}0.70$) but yields negligible improvement for the Random Forest classifier used throughout the main experiments ($\\Delta < 0.02$\\,pp), and even in the best case (logistic regression) leaves a residual gap relative to within-strategy performance (${\\sim}0.83$); a two-stage pipeline (strategy identification + strategy-conditioned classification) is therefore needed for reliable cross-strategy deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-unknown deployment.}\nIn strategy-unknown settings, naive transfer on d1--d3 yields only 45.9\\%, and d1/d2 predictions may invert.\nA two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp over LOO, though per-model gains vary (GPT-2~XL $+$16.4\\,pp vs.\\ Pythia $+$8.0\\,pp).\nPractical deployment requires either strategy metadata or learned routing.\n\n\\paragraph{d0 inflation of headline metrics.}\nDepth~0 (human-written text) is near-perfectly separable (recall $>$98\\% across all nine model--strategy settings), inflating headline accuracy.\nCross-model transfer accuracy of 66.4\\% is substantially driven by d0; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% in the worst case (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\paragraph{Controlled vs.\\ ecological setting.}\nThe primary benchmark uses Wikipedia seed text with controlled LoRA fine-tuning (1--1.5B models), a design that isolates the depth variable but diverges from wild contamination where models train on mixed corpora with prior-generation outputs at unknown proportions.\nDomain bias controls confirm depth as the dominant factor: LOGO accuracy (72.1\\%) matches standard CV (72.0\\%), seed-group prediction is at chance (10.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain_bias}).\nCross-scale experiments (7--8B, \\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}) show weaker but above-chance signal; a base-model control suggests instruction-tuning regularizes degradation.\nSegment-level estimation in mixed-depth documents remains open; in particular, partial mixing (accumulation), where a single document contains fragments from different recursive depths, is a key open question, as our framework assumes uniform depth per passage.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-dependent false positive rate.}\nOn editorial text (WikiText-103), all classifiers achieve $\\geq$98.5\\% d0 accuracy, but on web-crawl data (C4, 5{,}000 passages), per-model FPR ranges from 4.7\\% (OLMo) to 22.4\\% (GPT-2~XL).\nA pooled RF classifier evaluated on larger corpora (10{,}000 passages each) yields 34\\% FPR on C4 and 17\\% on FineWeb \\cite{penedo2024_fineweb}; the gap reflects quality filtering in FineWeb removing feature-anomalous text that triggers false positives.\nMisclassifications concentrate in template/SEO content with elevated repetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_3gram} $+$37\\%, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram} $+$75\\% vs.\\ correctly classified d0).\nDeployment on heterogeneous web corpora requires model- and domain-specific calibration (Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}).\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial robustness.}\nAdversarial paraphrasing \\cite{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} could disrupt the perplexity tail features that drive classification; depth estimation's robustness under such attacks is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD monotonicity assumption.}\nOur EDD definition (Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}) requires all pairwise AUCs up to depth $d^*$ to exceed $T$, which is well-behaved when AUC decays monotonically, as observed in all our configurations.\nHowever, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} show that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, which could produce non-monotonic AUC profiles (e.g., a dip at d1/d2 followed by recovery at d2/d3).\nOur cross-strategy transfer results provide an empirical example: training on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1-vs-d2 AUC as low as 0.129 (GPT-2~XL) and 0.253 (OLMo), violating the monotonicity assumption.\nIn such cases EDD would conservatively report a lower value than the deepest discriminable pair, potentially underestimating the true discriminable range; extending EDD to handle non-contiguous discriminable regions is future work.\nThe choice of threshold $T$ is also application-dependent: at $T{=}0.80$, several cells lose one depth level relative to $T{=}0.75$.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model dependency.}\nReference model choice affects classification accuracy by up to 11\\,pp; same-family references yield optimal performance, though all configurations exceed $2.5\\times$ chance.\nSurprisal-based features are computed relative to Pythia-1.4B, which coincides with one of the three generators.\nFor the Pythia pipeline this constitutes self-model feature extraction, while OLMo and GPT-2~XL measurements are cross-model.\nEmpirically, the self-model configuration does not yield the highest accuracy (Pythia 78.7\\% vs.\\ GPT-2~XL 85.9\\%); the overlap therefore does not confer a systematic advantage.\nAn ablation with OLMo-1B and GPT-2 XL as alternative references shows accuracy ranging from 67--79\\% across three reference architectures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}), all exceeding $2.5\\times$ chance; same-family reference yields optimal performance ($\\Delta{\\leq}$11\\,pp).\nThe choice of reference model affects feature values; a different reference model may shift absolute feature magnitudes and potentially alter discriminability at depth boundaries.\nReference-free surface features alone achieve 53.7\\% (partial signal in lexical statistics) but trail the full pipeline by 25pp; surprisal-based features are therefore essential.\nSimple multi-reference ensembling does not outperform the best single reference (73.7\\% vs.\\ 78.6\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Single LoRA instance.}\nIndependent replication with seed=43 yields 77.15\\% (95\\% CI [76.4, 78.15], $\\Delta{=}{-}1.05$\\,pp vs.\\ baseline); cross-seed transfer retains 76.76\\% ($\\Delta{=}{-}1.30$\\,pp vs.\\ within-seed avg); depth signal is thus inherent to the recursive process rather than specific LoRA weights (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).\nTop-5 feature importance is identical across seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Additional caveats.}\nDepth signal scales with passage length (68.4\\% at 128 tokens, 77.8\\% at 512; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_ablation}), saturating near 500 tokens.\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}, and curated synthetic data may not trigger collapse \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work uses publicly available pretrained language models (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and Wikipedia text as seed data.\nNo human subjects were involved, no private or sensitive data were collected, and no new models were released.\nOur method is intended to support data provenance and training-data auditing; we acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be misused to circumvent content moderation, though such risks are shared with all detection-oriented research.\nCode and data will be released upon acceptance.\nThis research used AI coding assistants for experiment implementation and writing drafts.\nAll scientific decisions, experimental design, and result interpretation were conducted by the authors.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether atlas accuracy (68.8--85.9\\%) reflects genuine statistical regularities across eight generalization axes (five below, three in Appendix).\n\n\\subsection{Classifier Comparison and Surface-Text Ablation}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} evaluates bagging (RF) and gradient boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM) on the same 19 features; per-cell spread ($\\leq$0.4pp) establishes a feature-determined ceiling.\nOn Pythia nucleus, CORAL \\cite{coral2020} (78.1\\%) and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} (78.2\\%) match RF within 0.6\\,pp ($p>0.05$); all feature-based methods outperform CE-MLP (74.9\\%) and logistic regression (75.0\\%).\nBinary detectors repurposed for ordinal classification (Binoculars + Fast-DetectGPT combined: 74.2\\%) fall 4\\,pp below RF (Appendix~\\ref{app:detector_baselines}).\n\n\\paragraph{Surface-text ablation.}\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%); zero-shot LLMs (DeepSeek V3.2, 36.2\\%; Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, 30.4\\%) also fail (43\\,pp below RF; Appendix~\\ref{app:zeroshot_prompt}); depth signal therefore resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Classifier convergence: 4-class accuracy (\\%) across three atlas cells. RF (bagging), XGBoost and LightGBM (gradient boosting) operate on the same 19 features; $\\Delta_{\\max}$: largest per-cell spread. Chance = 25\\%. RF values differ from Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} by $\\leq$0.2pp due to matched-fold evaluation. 5-fold cross-validation standard deviation $\\leq$ 0.9\\,pp across all settings.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & RF & XGB & LGBM & $\\Delta_{\\max}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia, nuc. & 78.8 & 79.0 & 78.9 & 0.2 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL, $\\tau$ & 85.8 & 86.2 & 86.2 & 0.4 \\\\\nPythia, top-$k$ & 68.9 & 68.9 & 68.7 & 0.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nConvergence confirms the signal resides in the features; three alternative classifiers match RF within 0.8\\,pp under cross-model transfer (Appendix~\\ref{app:learned_estimator}).\nPerplexity features alone recover 66.0\\% ($-$0.5\\,pp vs.\\ full), while repetition/diversity reach only 53.0\\% ($-$13.6\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_ablation}); perplexity degradation is the dominant depth marker.\nRaw perplexity alone performs at chance (25.1\\%); the strongest single feature (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) reaches 58.3\\% ($-$8.3\\,pp vs.\\ full); multi-dimensional characterization is required.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 near-perfectly identified; errors concentrate at d2/d3 in-distribution and spread to d1--d3 under transfer (seed std $= 0.06$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_seed}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.9\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d1--d3 bal.\\ acc: Pythia 49.9\\%, OLMo 59.2\\%, GPT-2~XL 57.2\\% (mean 55.4\\%).}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy is 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance); d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline (d1--d3 balanced mean 55.4\\%).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy} reports leave-one-strategy-out transfer.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d0 recall $\\geq$89\\% inflates headline; d1--d3 mean: 45.9\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7pp below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%); a 1.5B instruct control (47.4\\%, $1.9\\times$ chance) confirms at matched scale (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct_1p5b}).\nCross-model chains (84.75\\%), cross-domain (arXiv 74.1\\%), and LoRA rank sensitivity ($\\Delta{=}4.65$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) further confirm robustness.",
